ENTRY 0107 | UNDERGRADUATE | Ecology | Ecosystems
TOPIC: Food Chains Webs and Trophic Levels
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
1. DEFINITION
Analyzing how energy and matter move through ecosystems.

2. DEEP CONCEPTUAL EXPLANATION
Producers capture energy, consumers transfer it through trophic levels and decomposers recycle matter. Food webs represent multiple interacting pathways rather than a single chain.

3. CORE CONCEPTS
   - producer
   - primary consumer
   - secondary consumer
   - trophic level
   - food web

4. KEY EQUATION / QUANTITATIVE RELATIONSHIP
Trophic transfer efficiency is the proportion of energy passed to the next level.

5. GEOGRAPHIC APPLICATIONS
ecosystem mapping; wildlife ecology

ENTRY 0108 | UNDERGRADUATE | Ecology | Biogeochemical Cycles
TOPIC: Carbon Nitrogen and Phosphorus Cycles
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
1. DEFINITION
Understanding movement of key elements through atmosphere, organisms, soils, water and rocks.

2. DEEP CONCEPTUAL EXPLANATION
Carbon, nitrogen and phosphorus circulate through biological, geological and chemical processes. Human activity can accelerate or disrupt these cycles.

3. CORE CONCEPTS
   - carbon cycle
   - nitrogen cycle
   - phosphorus cycle
   - decomposition
   - nutrient cycling

4. KEY EQUATION / QUANTITATIVE RELATIONSHIP
Net ecosystem exchange can be conceptualized as carbon uptake minus ecosystem respiration and other losses.

5. GEOGRAPHIC APPLICATIONS
climate change; soil fertility; ecosystem management

ENTRY 0109 | UNDERGRADUATE | Environmental Geography | Degradation
TOPIC: Land Degradation Soil Erosion and Desertification
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
1. DEFINITION
Analyzing processes that reduce land productivity and ecosystem function.

2. DEEP CONCEPTUAL EXPLANATION
Land degradation can result from erosion, salinization, nutrient depletion, vegetation loss and unsustainable land use. Desertification refers to land degradation in dryland contexts and is not simply the expansion of deserts.

3. CORE CONCEPTS
   - erosion
   - salinization
   - nutrient loss
   - desertification
   - land degradation

4. KEY EQUATION / QUANTITATIVE RELATIONSHIP
Soil loss can be expressed as mass or depth lost per unit area per time.

5. GEOGRAPHIC APPLICATIONS
soil conservation; dryland management

------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
1. DEFINITION
Analyzing changing climate systems and associated environmental impacts.

2. DEEP CONCEPTUAL EXPLANATION
Climate change involves long-term shifts in temperature, precipitation, circulation and other variables. Sea level responds to thermal expansion, ice-sheet and glacier mass changes and other processes.

3. CORE CONCEPTS
   - greenhouse gases
   - warming
   - sea level
   - ice sheets
   - adaptation
   - mitigation

4. KEY EQUATION / QUANTITATIVE RELATIONSHIP

coastal planning; climate adaptation

ENTRY 0113 | UNDERGRADUATE | Remote Sensing | Fundamentals
TOPIC: Electromagnetic Spectrum and Atmospheric Windows
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
1. DEFINITION
Understanding electromagnetic radiation used in remote sensing and atmospheric transmission.

2. DEEP CONCEPTUAL EXPLANATION
Sensors detect energy in selected wavelength ranges. Atmospheric gases absorb some wavelengths and transmit others, creating atmospheric windows useful for observation.

3. CORE CONCEPTS
   - electromagnetic spectrum
   - wavelength
   - reflectance
   - absorption
   - atmospheric window

4. KEY EQUATION / QUANTITATIVE RELATIONSHIP
c = λf relates wavelength, frequency and propagation speed in vacuum.

5. GEOGRAPHIC APPLICATIONS
satellite imaging; spectral analysis

ENTRY 0114 | UNDERGRADUATE | Remote Sensing | Sensors
TOPIC: Passive and Active Remote Sensing
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
1. DEFINITION
Distinguishing sensors that measure naturally available energy from sensors that emit their own energy.

2. DEEP CONCEPTUAL EXPLANATION
Passive sensors commonly detect reflected solar or emitted thermal radiation. Active sensors such as radar or lidar transmit energy and measure the returned signal.

3. CORE CONCEPTS
   - passive
   - active
   - radar
   - lidar
   - thermal
   - reflectance

4. KEY EQUATION / QUANTITATIVE RELATIONSHIP
Returned signal strength depends on transmitted energy, target properties, geometry and atmospheric effects.

5. GEOGRAPHIC APPLICATIONS
SAR mapping; optical imagery; elevation mapping

ENTRY 0115 | UNDERGRADUATE | Remote Sensing | Image Analysis
TOPIC: Spatial Spectral Radiometric Temporal Resolution
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
1. DEFINITION
Understanding the four major dimensions of image resolution.

2. DEEP CONCEPTUAL EXPLANATION
Spatial resolution describes ground detail, spectral resolution describes wavelength discrimination, radiometric resolution describes sensitivity to energy differences and temporal resolution describes revisit frequency.

3. CORE CONCEPTS
   - spatial
   - spectral
   - radiometric
   - temporal
   - resolution

4. KEY EQUATION / QUANTITATIVE RELATIONSHIP
Pixel ground area depends on spatial resolution in the relevant dimensions.

5. GEOGRAPHIC APPLICATIONS
sensor selection; change detection; land-cover mapping

6. TWENTY EXAMPLES
   1. Definition example: define Spatial Spectral Radiometric Temporal Resolution and identify its main geographic question.
   2. Local example: identify a real-world occurrence of Spatial Spectral Radiometric Temporal Resolution in a familiar landscape.

ENTRY 0116 | UNDERGRADUATE | Remote Sensing | Image Interpretation
TOPIC: Spectral Signatures and FCC
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
1. DEFINITION
Using spectral response patterns and false-color composites to interpret Earth surfaces.

2. DEEP CONCEPTUAL EXPLANATION
Vegetation, water and soil reflect or absorb electromagnetic energy differently. False-color composites assign bands to display channels to emphasize particular features.

3. CORE CONCEPTS
   - spectral signature
   - vegetation
   - water
   - soil
   - FCC

4. KEY EQUATION / QUANTITATIVE RELATIONSHIP
Spectral reflectance = reflected energy / incident energy for a wavelength band.

5. GEOGRAPHIC APPLICATIONS
vegetation mapping; water mapping; land-cover interpretation

ENTRY 0117 | UNDERGRADUATE | GIS | Data Models
TOPIC: Vector and Raster Data
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
1. DEFINITION
Representing geographic information as points, lines, polygons or grid cells.

2. DEEP CONCEPTUAL EXPLANATION
Vector models are effective for discrete objects and precise boundaries; raster models represent continuous fields naturally and support cell-based operations. Choice depends on phenomenon and analysis.

3. CORE CONCEPTS
   - point
   - line
   - polygon
   - raster
   - cell
   - attribute

4. KEY EQUATION / QUANTITATIVE RELATIONSHIP
Raster cell count = rows × columns.

------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Representing relationships such as adjacency, connectivity and containment among geographic features.

2. DEEP CONCEPTUAL EXPLANATION
Topology prevents certain geometric inconsistencies and enables network and polygon analyses. Common relationships include connected, adjacent and within.

   - adjacency
   - connectivity
   - containment
   - intersection
   - topology

4. KEY EQUATION / QUANTITATIVE RELATIONSHIP
Topology can be represented as a graph of nodes, edges and faces depending on data type.

ENTRY 0119 | UNDERGRADUATE | GIS | Analysis
TOPIC: Buffer Overlay Intersection Union Identity
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
1. DEFINITION
Applying common GIS spatial-analysis operations to combine or transform geographic layers.

2. DEEP CONCEPTUAL EXPLANATION
Buffering creates zones around features; overlay operations combine spatial and attribute information. Intersection retains overlap, union combines polygon coverage, and identity transfers attributes according to spatial relationships.

3. CORE CONCEPTS
   - buffer
   - overlay
   - intersection
   - union
   - identity

4. KEY EQUATION / QUANTITATIVE RELATIONSHIP
Buffer area around a point under an ideal circle = πr².

5. GEOGRAPHIC APPLICATIONS
service areas; hazard zones; land-use planning

ENTRY 0120 | UNDERGRADUATE | GNSS | Positioning
TOPIC: GPS GLONASS Galileo NavIC
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
1. DEFINITION
Understanding satellite navigation systems used to determine geographic position.

2. DEEP CONCEPTUAL EXPLANATION
GNSS receivers estimate position from signals from multiple satellites. GPS, GLONASS, Galileo and NavIC are different systems or constellations providing positioning services.

3. CORE CONCEPTS
   - GNSS
   - GPS
   - GLONASS
   - Galileo
   - NavIC
   - receiver

4. KEY EQUATION / QUANTITATIVE RELATIONSHIP
Positioning uses ranges to satellites; trilateration solves for receiver location and timing.

field mapping; navigation; ground truthing

ENTRY 0121 | UNDERGRADUATE | GIS | Terrain
TOPIC: DEM DTM Slope and Aspect
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
1. DEFINITION
Analyzing elevation surfaces and terrain derivatives in GIS.

2. DEEP CONCEPTUAL EXPLANATION
Digital elevation models represent elevation values across a spatial grid or surface. Slope measures steepness, while aspect represents direction of maximum descent or related terrain orientation.

3. CORE CONCEPTS
   - DEM
   - DTM
   - slope
   - aspect
   - elevation
   - terrain

4. KEY EQUATION / QUANTITATIVE RELATIONSHIP
Slope magnitude can be derived from elevation gradients in x and y directions.

watershed modeling; hazard mapping; terrain analysis

ENTRY 0125 | UNDERGRADUATE | Regional Planning | Regions
TOPIC: Formal Functional and Perceptual Regions
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
1. DEFINITION
Distinguishing regions defined by uniform characteristics, functional connections or perceived identity.

2. DEEP CONCEPTUAL EXPLANATION
Formal regions share selected attributes, functional regions are organized around flows or nodes, and perceptual regions arise from people's mental or cultural understanding.

3. CORE CONCEPTS
   - formal
   - functional
   - perceptual
   - nodal
   - region

4. KEY EQUATION / QUANTITATIVE RELATIONSHIP

TOPIC: Absolute Relative and Relational Space
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
1. DEFINITION
Comparing different geographical conceptions of space.

2. DEEP CONCEPTUAL EXPLANATION
Absolute space treats locations as fixed coordinates; relative space emphasizes distance and accessibility; relational space focuses on connections, networks and socially produced relationships.

3. CORE CONCEPTS
   - absolute
   - relative
   - relational
   - distance
   - network

4. KEY EQUATION / QUANTITATIVE RELATIONSHIP
Relative distance can be measured by travel time or generalized cost rather than kilometers alone.

ENTRY 0127 | UNDERGRADUATE | Regional Planning | Growth
TOPIC: Growth Pole Theory
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
1. DEFINITION
Explaining regional development through concentration around dynamic economic poles.

2. DEEP CONCEPTUAL EXPLANATION
Perroux's growth-pole concept emphasizes industries or activities that generate growth impulses and linkages. Geographic implementation depends on actual spatial concentration and institutional conditions.

3. CORE CONCEPTS
   - growth pole
   - linkages
   - propulsive industry
   - regional development

4. KEY EQUATION / QUANTITATIVE RELATIONSHIP
Growth multiplier effects can be modeled as changes in induced activity relative to initial investment.

5. GEOGRAPHIC APPLICATIONS
industrial corridors; regional planning

------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Analyzing uneven regional development between dominant cores and dependent or peripheral areas.

2. DEEP CONCEPTUAL EXPLANATION
Friedmann's framework highlights concentration of decision-making, investment and innovation in core regions and weaker connectivity or dependence in peripheral regions.

   - core
   - periphery
   - development
   - dependency
   - flow

4. KEY EQUATION / QUANTITATIVE RELATIONSHIP
Core-periphery inequality can be measured through income, accessibility or investment ratios.

ENTRY 0129 | UNDERGRADUATE | Regional Planning | Cumulative Causation
TOPIC: Myrdal's Cumulative Causation
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
1. DEFINITION
Explaining how initial advantages can reinforce further growth or decline.

2. DEEP CONCEPTUAL EXPLANATION
Myrdal emphasized circular cumulative processes in which investment, skills and opportunities attract more activity, while disadvantaged areas can experience backwash effects unless counteracting spread effects occur.

3. CORE CONCEPTS
   - cumulative causation
   - backwash
   - spread
   - circular process

4. KEY EQUATION / QUANTITATIVE RELATIONSHIP
Growth feedback can be represented as change in one variable influencing subsequent changes in related variables.

5. GEOGRAPHIC APPLICATIONS
regional disparities; development policy

ENTRY 0132 | UNDERGRADUATE | Field Research | Observation

------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
1. DEFINITION
Collecting systematic observations along a route through a landscape.

2. DEEP CONCEPTUAL EXPLANATION
Transects reveal changes in land use, vegetation, soil, slope and human activity across space. Standardized observation sheets improve comparability.

3. CORE CONCEPTS
   - transect
   - observation
   - land use
   - ground truth
   - field notes

4. KEY EQUATION / QUANTITATIVE RELATIONSHIP

ENTRY 0133 | UNDERGRADUATE | Field Research | Ground Truthing
TOPIC: Ground Truthing Satellite Images
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
1. DEFINITION
Comparing remote-sensing classifications with observed conditions on the ground.

2. DEEP CONCEPTUAL EXPLANATION
Ground truthing validates image interpretation and classification by linking pixels or polygons to real-world land-cover conditions. Sampling should represent different classes and uncertainty areas.

3. CORE CONCEPTS
   - ground truth
   - reference data
   - classification
   - accuracy
   - field GPS

4. KEY EQUATION / QUANTITATIVE RELATIONSHIP
Overall accuracy = correctly classified reference samples / total reference samples.

5. GEOGRAPHIC APPLICATIONS
land-cover mapping; change detection

ENTRY 0134 | UNDERGRADUATE | Research Methods | Data Processing
TOPIC: Coding and Cross-Tabulation
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
1. DEFINITION
Converting field observations into structured variables and comparing categories.

2. DEEP CONCEPTUAL EXPLANATION
Coding transforms qualitative or categorical observations into analyzable representations. Cross-tabulation examines relationships between categorical variables and can reveal spatial or social patterns.

3. CORE CONCEPTS
   - coding
   - variable
   - category
   - cross-tabulation
   - frequency

4. KEY EQUATION / QUANTITATIVE RELATIONSHIP
Row percentage = cell count / row total × 100%.

5. GEOGRAPHIC APPLICATIONS
survey analysis; field research; social geography

ENTRY 0136 | ADVANCED | Soil Geography | Pedology
TOPIC: Soil Formation Factors and Jenny's State Factor Model
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
1. DEFINITION
Explaining soil formation through climate, organisms, relief, parent material and time.

2. DEEP CONCEPTUAL EXPLANATION
Jenny formalized the idea that soil properties can be expressed as a function of state factors. The supplied formulation is S = f(C,O,R,P,T). Soil is dynamic and varies spatially because these factors vary.

3. CORE CONCEPTS
   - climate
   - organisms
   - relief
   - parent material
   - time
   - pedogenesis

4. KEY EQUATION / QUANTITATIVE RELATIONSHIP
S = f(C,O,R,P,T) is a conceptual state-factor relationship, not a single predictive equation.

5. GEOGRAPHIC APPLICATIONS
soil survey; land capability; agriculture

6. TWENTY EXAMPLES
   1. Definition example: define Soil Formation Factors and Jenny's State Factor Model and identify its main geographic question.

ENTRY 0137 | ADVANCED | Soil Geography | Taxonomy
TOPIC: USDA Soil Orders
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
1. DEFINITION
Recognizing major USDA Soil Taxonomy orders highlighted in the supplied material.

2. DEEP CONCEPTUAL EXPLANATION
Mollisols are associated with grassland-derived dark surface horizons; Oxisols are highly weathered tropical soils; Aridisols occur in arid environments; Histosols are organic-rich; Gelisols involve permafrost conditions.

3. CORE CONCEPTS
   - Mollisol
   - Oxisol
   - Aridisol
   - Histosol
   - Gelisol

4. KEY EQUATION / QUANTITATIVE RELATIONSHIP
Soil classification uses diagnostic horizons and properties rather than climate alone.

soil taxonomy; global soil comparison

ENTRY 0138 | ADVANCED | Biogeography | Biomes
TOPIC: Tropical Rainforest Ecology
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
1. DEFINITION
Analyzing structure, biodiversity and adaptations in tropical rainforest systems.

2. DEEP CONCEPTUAL EXPLANATION
High rainfall and warm temperatures support dense vegetation. Vertical stratification creates different light environments, while organisms show adaptations such as epiphytes, lianas, buttress roots and arboreal lifestyles.

3. CORE CONCEPTS
   - emergent
   - canopy
   - understory
   - epiphyte
   - liana
   - biodiversity

4. KEY EQUATION / QUANTITATIVE RELATIONSHIP
Net primary productivity can be compared as biomass accumulation per area per time.

biome comparison; wildlife ecology; carbon cycling

1. DEFINITION
Analyzing cold-climate coniferous forests and associated adaptations.

2. DEEP CONCEPTUAL EXPLANATION
Boreal forests are dominated by conifers adapted to cold conditions, short growing seasons and snow. Wildlife adaptations include insulation, migration, hibernation and seasonal coat changes.

3. CORE CONCEPTS
   - spruce
   - fir
   - pine
   - snow
   - permafrost
   - hibernation

4. KEY EQUATION / QUANTITATIVE RELATIONSHIP
Growing-season length can be compared using temperature thresholds and accumulated thermal time.

5. GEOGRAPHIC APPLICATIONS
climate-biomass studies; wildlife ecology

6. TWENTY EXAMPLES

ENTRY 0141 | ADVANCED | Zoogeography | Faunal Realms
TOPIC: Wallacean Zoogeography
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
1. DEFINITION
Studying large-scale animal distribution using biogeographic realms.

2. DEEP CONCEPTUAL EXPLANATION
The supplied material highlights Palearctic, Nearctic, Neotropical, Afrotropical and Australian realms. Historical isolation, climate, barriers and evolutionary history shape realm boundaries and species composition.

3. CORE CONCEPTS
   - Palearctic
   - Nearctic
   - Neotropical
   - Afrotropical
   - Australian

4. KEY EQUATION / QUANTITATIVE RELATIONSHIP
Realm similarity can be compared using species composition or endemism metrics.

faunal mapping; biogeography

ENTRY 0142 | ADVANCED | Biogeography | Barriers
TOPIC: Wallace's Line and Island Endemism
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
1. DEFINITION
Explaining how geographic isolation produces distinctive faunal assemblages.

2. DEEP CONCEPTUAL EXPLANATION
Wallace's Line marks a strong biogeographic transition in the Indonesian region. Islands can accumulate endemic species because gene flow is restricted and populations evolve independently.

3. CORE CONCEPTS
   - Wallace's Line
   - endemism
   - island biogeography
   - isolation
   - gene flow

4. KEY EQUATION / QUANTITATIVE RELATIONSHIP
Endemism rate = endemic species / total species × 100%.

5. GEOGRAPHIC APPLICATIONS
island conservation; evolutionary geography

ENTRY 0143 | ADVANCED | Ecology | Population Ecology
TOPIC: Carrying Capacity
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
1. DEFINITION
Understanding the maximum population size an environment can sustainably support under specified conditions.

2. DEEP CONCEPTUAL EXPLANATION
Carrying capacity is dynamic rather than a universal fixed number. Resource availability, predation, disease, climate and human intervention can alter the sustainable population level.

3. CORE CONCEPTS
   - K
   - resource limits
   - population growth
   - density dependence
   - sustainability

4. KEY EQUATION / QUANTITATIVE RELATIONSHIP
Logistic growth is often represented dN/dt = rN(1−N/K).

5. GEOGRAPHIC APPLICATIONS
wildlife management; rangelands; resource planning

ENTRY 0144 | ADVANCED | Ecology | Food Webs
TOPIC: Trophic Cascades
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
1. DEFINITION
Analyzing how changes at higher trophic levels propagate through food webs.

2. DEEP CONCEPTUAL EXPLANATION
Predators can alter herbivore abundance or behavior, which can affect vegetation and ecosystem processes. The supplied Yellowstone example illustrates a trophic-cascade argument, but outcomes are context dependent.

3. CORE CONCEPTS
   - predator
   - herbivore
   - producer
   - cascade
   - behavior
   - ecosystem

4. KEY EQUATION / QUANTITATIVE RELATIONSHIP
A trophic effect can be represented as changes in abundance or interaction strength across levels.

5. GEOGRAPHIC APPLICATIONS
rewilding; wildlife management

ENTRY 0145 | ADVANCED | Environmental Geography | Pollution
TOPIC: Biomagnification
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
1. DEFINITION
Understanding increasing concentrations of persistent contaminants at higher trophic levels.

2. DEEP CONCEPTUAL EXPLANATION
Persistent substances can accumulate in organisms and become more concentrated in predators because of feeding relationships. Bioaccumulation within an organism differs from biomagnification across trophic levels.

3. CORE CONCEPTS
   - bioaccumulation
   - biomagnification
   - persistent pollutant
   - trophic level

4. KEY EQUATION / QUANTITATIVE RELATIONSHIP
Biomagnification factor can be expressed as concentration at higher trophic level / concentration at lower level.

5. GEOGRAPHIC APPLICATIONS
mercury; persistent pesticides; food-web studies

ENTRY 0146 | ADVANCED | Environmental Geography | Land Change
TOPIC: Deforestation and Habitat Fragmentation
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
1. DEFINITION
Analyzing the removal and subdivision of natural habitats by human land-use change.

2. DEEP CONCEPTUAL EXPLANATION
Fragmentation reduces continuous habitat, changes edge conditions and can restrict gene flow. Effects vary with species, fragment size, connectivity and surrounding land use.

3. CORE CONCEPTS
   - deforestation
   - fragmentation
   - edge effect
   - connectivity
   - gene flow

4. KEY EQUATION / QUANTITATIVE RELATIONSHIP
Fragmentation can be quantified using patch number, mean patch size, edge density or connectivity indices.

5. GEOGRAPHIC APPLICATIONS
forest conservation; landscape ecology

ENTRY 0147 | ADVANCED | Environmental Geography | Soil Degradation
TOPIC: Soil Erosion Salinization and Desertification
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
1. DEFINITION
Analyzing processes that reduce soil productivity and land function.

2. DEEP CONCEPTUAL EXPLANATION
Water and wind erosion remove soil, salinization accumulates salts under particular water and drainage conditions, and dryland degradation can emerge through interacting climatic and land-use pressures.

3. CORE CONCEPTS
   - soil loss
   - salinity
   - erosion
   - desertification
   - land degradation

4. KEY EQUATION / QUANTITATIVE RELATIONSHIP
Soil erosion rate = soil mass lost / area / time.

5. GEOGRAPHIC APPLICATIONS
soil conservation; dryland management

ENTRY 0148 | ADVANCED | Human Ecology | Population Environment
TOPIC: Human-Environment Interaction
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
1. DEFINITION
Analyzing reciprocal relationships between human populations and natural systems.

2. DEEP CONCEPTUAL EXPLANATION
Humans modify land, water, energy and biogeochemical cycles while environmental conditions shape livelihoods, hazards and opportunities. Modern geography emphasizes feedbacks rather than one-way environmental control.

3. CORE CONCEPTS
   - anthrosphere
   - resource use
   - feedback
   - adaptation
   - impact

4. KEY EQUATION / QUANTITATIVE RELATIONSHIP
Ecological footprint and per-capita resource use are examples of human-pressure indicators.

5. GEOGRAPHIC APPLICATIONS
sustainability; environmental planning

6. TWENTY EXAMPLES
   1. Definition example: define Human-Environment Interaction and identify its main geographic question.
   2. Local example: identify a real-world occurrence of Human-Environment Interaction in a familiar landscape.

7. COMMON MISCONCEPTIONS
   - Human-Environment Interaction can be understood from a single factor alone.
   - A map, model or classification represents reality perfectly.
   - Correlation automatically proves causation.
   - A regional average describes every location within the region.
   - A geographic pattern is automatically a geographic process.
   - Human influence is always either entirely positive or entirely negative.

8. PREREQUISITES
Useful prerequisites: anthrosphere, resource use, feedback, adaptation.

9. RESEARCH / ETHICAL / FIELD CONTEXT
Handle demographic, cultural and social data carefully; avoid stereotyping groups and protect personally identifiable survey information.

10. LEARNING DEPTH
This entry should be understood at the advanced level while connecting its concepts to spatial pattern, process, scale, evidence and human-environment relationships.
